---
<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
The \textit{Cartesian product} of graphs $G$ and $H$, denoted by $G \Box H$, is the graph where $V(G \Box H) = V(G) \times V(H)$ and $E(G \Box H) = \{((g,h),(g',h')) : g = g' \text{ and } (h,h') \in E(H), \text{ or } h = h' \text{ and }(g,g')\in E(G)\}$.
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{theorem:intro_homology}
		Let $n\ge 2$. Then 
		\begin{enumerate}[label = (\roman*)] 
			\item \label{theorem:intro_homology_i} $\tilde{H}_i(M(G_{3\times n})) = 0$ for $i \leq n-2$ and $i = \dim(M(G_{3\times n})) $.
			\item \label{theorem:intro_homology_ii} $\tilde{H}_{n-1}(M(G_{3\times n}))\ne0$.
		\end{enumerate}
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{theorem:intro_simply_connected}
		For $n\ge3$, $M(G_{3\times n})$ is simply connected.
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
For $n \geq 3$, conn$(M(G_{3\times n})) = n-2$.
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- definition-2: body_md -->
Let $G$ and $H$ be simple graphs (\textit{i.e.}, undirected graphs with no loops or multiple edges).
		An \textit{isomorphism} from $G$ to $H$ is a bijection $f:V(G)\rightarrow V(H)$ such that, for every $u,v\in V(G),$ $(u,v)\in E(G)$ if and only if $(f(u),f(v))\in E(H).$ If such an isomorphism exists, then $G$ and $H$ are said to be \textit{isomorphic}, and it is denoted by $G\cong H.$
<!-- end definition-2 -->
<!-- definition-3: body_md -->
Let $\Delta_1$ and $\Delta_2$ be two abstract simplicial complexes with disjoint vertex sets. The \textit{join} of $\Delta_1$ and $\Delta_2$ is the abstract simplicial complex $\Delta_1 * \Delta_2$, whose vertex set is $V (\Delta_1) \cup V (\Delta_2)$ and whose set of simplices is given by
		$$\Delta_1 * \Delta_2 = \{\sigma\subseteq V (\Delta_1) \cup V (\Delta_2) \ | \ \sigma\cap V (\Delta_1) \in \Delta_1\text{ and }\sigma\cap V (\Delta_2) \in \Delta_2\}.$$
<!-- end definition-3 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{theorem:lk_del} 
		Let $\Delta$ be a simplicial complex and $v$ be a vertex of $\Delta$. If $lk(v,\Delta)$ is contractible in $del(v,\Delta)$, then 
		$$\Delta\simeq del(v,\Delta)\vee \Sigma(lk(v,\Delta)).$$
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- corollary-2: body_md -->
\label{corollary:N[u]subsetN[v]_lk(v)_del(v)} 
		Let $G$ be a graph and $u,v\in V(G), u\neq v$ such that $N_{G}[u]\subseteq N_{G}[v]$. Then
		$$\text{Ind}(G)\simeq \text{Ind}(G-\{v\})\vee \Sigma(\text{Ind}(G-N_G[v])).$$
<!-- end corollary-2 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{theorem:N(u)subsetN(v)}
		Let $G$ be a graph and $u,v\in V(G), u\neq v$ such that $N_{G}(u)\subseteq N_{G}(v)$. Then
		$$\text{Ind}(G)\simeq \text{Ind}(G-\{v\}).$$
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{theorem:simplicial_vertex}
		Let $v$ be a simplicial vertex of a graph $G$ and $N_{G}(v) = \{w_1,\cdots, w_k\}$. Then
		$$\text{Ind}(G)\simeq \bigvee^{k}_{i = 1}\Sigma(\text{Ind}(G-N_{G}[w_i])).$$
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{lemma:edge_contractible}
		Let $G$ be a graph and $\{u,v\}$ be a $1$-simplex in $\text{Ind}(G)$. If $\text{Ind}(G-N_G[\{u,v\}])$ is contractible, then
		$$\text{Ind}(G)\simeq \text{Ind}(G'),$$
		where $V(G') = V(G)$ and $E(G') = E(G)\cup \{(u,v)\}.$
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{lemma:ind_disjoint_union}
		Let $G_1\sqcup G_2$ denote the disjoint union of two graphs $G_1$ and $G_2$. Then
		$$\text{Ind}(G_1\sqcup G_2)\simeq \text{Ind}(G_1) *\text{Ind}(G_2).$$
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- corollary-3: body_md -->
\label{corollary:isolated_vertex}
		Let $G\sqcup \{v\}$ denote the disjoint union of a graph $G$ with an isolated vertex $v$. Then $\text{Ind}(G\sqcup \{v\})$ is contractible.
<!-- end corollary-3 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{theorem:replace_edge}
		Let $G$ be a graph and $e = (u,v)$ an edge. If $G'$ is obtained from $G$ by replacing $e$ with a path $uabcv$ with three new vertices, then $\text{Ind}(G')\simeq \Sigma(\text{Ind}(G)).$
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
\label{lemma:path_graph}
		For $n\geq 1,$
		$$\text{Ind}(P_n)\simeq 
		\begin{cases}
			\mathbb{S}^{k-1}& \text{ if } n = 3k,\\
			\text{pt} & \text{ if } n = 3k+1,\\
			\mathbb{S}^{k} & \text{ if } n = 3k+2.
		\end{cases}$$
		where pt denotes a space with one point.
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label{theorem:cycle_graph}
		For $n\geq 3,$
		$$\text{Ind}(C_n)\simeq 
		\begin{cases}
			\mathbb{S}^{k-1}\vee \mathbb{S}^{k-1} & \text{ if } n = 3k,\\
			\mathbb{S}^{k-1} & \text{ if } n = 3k\pm 1.
		\end{cases}$$
<!-- end lemma-8 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{theorem:ind_homology}
		Let $n\ge 2$. Then 
		\begin{enumerate}[label = (\roman*)] 
			\item \label{theorem:ind_homology_i} $\tilde{H}_i(\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n)) = 0$ for $i \leq n-2$ and $i = \dim(\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n)) $.
			\item \label{theorem:ind_homology_ii} $\tilde{H}_{n-1}(\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n))\ne0$.
		\end{enumerate}
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- claim-1: body_md -->
\label{claim:Un_relation}
		For $n\ge3$,
		$$\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_n)\simeq
		\begin{cases}
			\vee_4\mathbb{S}^2 &\text{ if $n = 3$},\\
			\Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_{n-1}))\vee \Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Z}_{n-3}))\vee \Sigma^3(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_{n-3})) &\text{ if $n\ge4$}.
		\end{cases}$$
<!-- end claim-1 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
Note that for $n\ge3$, $u$ is a simplicial vertex in $\mathcal{U}_n$ with $N_{\mathcal{U}_n}(u) = \{d_1, e_1\}$ (see \Cref{fig:Un_2}). By \Cref{theorem:simplicial_vertex}, $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_n)\simeq \Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_n-N_{\mathcal{U}_n}[d_1]))\vee \Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_n-N_{\mathcal{U}_n}[e_1]))$.
		We have $\mathcal{U}_n-N_{\mathcal{U}_n}[d_1]\cong \mathcal{U}_{n-1}$ (see \Cref{fig:Un-N_d1}). Let $\mathcal{U}_n': = \mathcal{U}_n-N_{\mathcal{U}_n}[e_1]$. Then $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_n)\simeq \Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_{n-1}))\vee\Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_n'))$.

		For $n = 3$, $\mathcal{U}_3'\cong \mathcal{U}_2$. Hence $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_3)\simeq \vee_2\Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_2))\simeq \vee_4 \mathbb{S}^2$ by \eqref{equation:U_12}. 
		
		Now, let $n\ge4$. Since $N_{\mathcal{U}_n'}(b_1)\subseteq N_{\mathcal{U}_n'}(b_2)$, \Cref{theorem:N(u)subsetN(v)} implies $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_n')\simeq \text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_n'-\{b_2\})$. Note that $(\mathcal{U}_n'-\{b_2\})-N_{\mathcal{U}_n'-\{b_2\}}[\{a_1,b_3\}]$ contains an isolated vertex $c_1$ (\Cref{fig:Un'_b2}), and hence $\text{Ind}((\mathcal{U}_n'-\{b_2\})-N_{\mathcal{U}_n'-\{b_2\}}[\{a_1,b_3\}])$ is contractible. By \Cref{lemma:edge_contractible}, $ \text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_n'-\{b_2\})\simeq \text{Ind}((\mathcal{U}_n'-\{b_2\})\cup \{(a_1,b_3)\})$. Let $\mathcal{U}_n'': = (\mathcal{U}_n'-\{b_2\})\cup \{(a_1,b_3)\}$ (see \Cref{fig:U_n''}). Then $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_n')\simeq \text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_n'')$, which implies $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_n)\simeq\Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_{n-1}))\vee \Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_n''))$. 
		
		Since $N_{\mathcal{U}_n''}[a_2]\subseteq N_{\mathcal{U}_n''}[b_3]$, \Cref{corollary:N[u]subsetN[v]_lk(v)_del(v)} implies $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_n'')\simeq \text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_n''-\{b_3\})\vee\Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_n''-N_{\mathcal{U}_n''}[b_3]))$. Observe that $\mathcal{U}_n''-N_{\mathcal{U}_n''}[b_3]\cong\mathcal{U}_{n-3}\sqcup P_2$ (\Cref{fig:U_n''-N_b3}) and $\mathcal{U}_n''-\{b_3\}\cong\mathcal{Z}_{n-3}$ (\Cref{fig_U_n''-b3}).
		Therefore $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_n'')\simeq \text{Ind}(\mathcal{Z}_{n-3})\vee \Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_{n-3}\sqcup P_2))$.

				\begin{tikzpicture}[scale = 0.27, vertices/.style = {draw, fill = black, circle, inner sep = 0.5pt}]
					
					                       
					\node[vertices,label = above:{{\tiny $b_1$}}] (b1) at (17, 3) {};

		By \Cref{lemma:ind_disjoint_union}, $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_{n-3}\sqcup P_2)\simeq \text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_{n-3})* \text{Ind}(P_2) \simeq \Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_{n-3}))$. It follows that $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_n'')\simeq \text{Ind}(\mathcal{Z}_{n-3}) \vee \Sigma^2 (\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_{n-3}))$. Thus $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_n)\simeq\Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_{n-1}))\vee \Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Z}_{n-3}))\vee \Sigma^3(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_{n-3})).$

\label{claim:Vn_relation}
		For $n\ge3$,
		$\text{Ind}(\mathcal{V}_n)\simeq \Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_{n-2}))\vee \Sigma^2 (\text{Ind}(\Gamma_{n-1})).$
<!-- end claim-2 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
For $n\ge3$, $v_2$ is a simplicial vertex in $\mathcal{V}_n$ with $N_{\mathcal{V}_n}(v_2) = \{a_1, b_1\}$ (see \Cref{fig:Vn_2}). Using \Cref{theorem:simplicial_vertex}, we get $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{V}_n)\simeq \Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{V}_n-N_{\mathcal{V}_n}[a_1]))\vee \Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{V}_n-N_{\mathcal{V}_n}[b_1]))$.
		Let $\mathcal{V}_n': = \mathcal{V}_n-N_{\mathcal{V}_n}[a_1]$ and $\mathcal{V}_n'': = \mathcal{V}_n-N_{\mathcal{V}_n}[b_1]$. Then $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{V}_n)\simeq \Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{V}_n'))\vee \Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{V}_n''))$.

		Since $N_{\mathcal{V}_n'}(v_1)\subseteq N_{\mathcal{V}_n'}(d_1)$, $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{V}_ n')\simeq \text{Ind}(\mathcal{V}_n'-\{d_1\})$ by \Cref{theorem:N(u)subsetN(v)}. Note that $\mathcal{V}_n'-\{d_1\}\cong\mathcal{X}_{n-2}$. Therefore $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{V}_n')\simeq \text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_{n-2})$. 
		Further, $v_1$ is a simplicial vertex in $\mathcal{V}_n''$ with $N_{\mathcal{V}_n''}(v_1) = \{v_4\}$. Hence $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{V}_n'')\simeq \Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{V}_n''-N_{\mathcal{V}_n''}[v_4]))$. Since $\mathcal{V}_n''-N_{\mathcal{V}_n''}[v_4]\cong\Gamma_{n-1}$, we have $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{V}_n'')\simeq \Sigma (\text{Ind}(\Gamma_{n-1}))$. Thus $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{V}_n)\simeq \Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_{n-2}))\vee \Sigma^2 (\text{Ind}(\Gamma_{n-1})).$

<!-- claim-3: body_md -->
\label{claim:Wn_relation}
		For $n\ge3$, let $W_n': = \mathcal{W}_n-\{d_1\}$. Then $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_n)\simeq \text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_n')$. Moreover, 
		$$lk(w_1,\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_n'))\simeq 
		\begin{cases} 
			\vee_3\mathbb{S}^2& \text{ if $n = 3$,}\\
			\Sigma^2 (\text{Ind}(\Gamma_{n-2}))\vee \Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_{n-3})) & \text{ if $n\ge4$,}
		\end{cases}$$ 
		and $del(w_1,\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_n'))\simeq \text{Ind}(\mathcal{Y}_{n-1}).$
<!-- end claim-3 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
Since $N_{\mathcal{W}_n}(w_1)\subseteq N_{\mathcal{W}_n}(d_1)$ (\Cref{fig:Wn_2}), $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_n)\simeq \text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_n-\{d_1\})$. Hence $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_n)\simeq \text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_n')$.
		
		We first compute the deletion of the vertex $w_1$ in $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_n')$. Using \eqref{equation:lk_del_ind_complex}, $del(w_1,\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_n'))\simeq \text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_n'-\{w_1\})$. Since $\mathcal{W}_n'-\{w_1\}\cong \mathcal{Y}_{n-1}$ (\Cref{fig:Wn'-w1}), it follows that $del(w_1,\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_n'))\simeq \text{Ind}(\mathcal{Y}_{n-1}).$

		For the link, we have $lk(w_1,\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_n'))\simeq \text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_n'-N_{\mathcal{W}_n'}[w_1])$ by \eqref{equation:lk_del_ind_complex}. Let $\mathcal{W}_n'': = \mathcal{W}_n'-N_{\mathcal{W}_n'}[w_1]$ (see \Cref{fig:Wn''}). Then $lk(w_1,\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_n'))\simeq \text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_n'')$. Since $e_1$ is a simplicial vertex in $\mathcal{W}_n''$ with $N_{\mathcal{W}_n''}(e_1) = \{d_2,e_2\}$, \Cref{theorem:simplicial_vertex} implies $\text{Ind}( \mathcal{W}_n'')\simeq \Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_n''-N_{\mathcal{W}_n''}[d_2]))\vee \Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_n''-N_{\mathcal{W}_n''}[e_2]))$. 
		
		We first assume $n = 3$. Then $\mathcal{W}_3''-N_{\mathcal{W}_3''}[d_2]\cong P_5$ and $\mathcal{W}_3''-N_{\mathcal{W}_3''}[e_2]\cong \Gamma_2$. It follows that $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_3'')\simeq\Sigma(\text{Ind}(P_5))\vee\Sigma(\text{Ind}(\Gamma_2))$, and therefore $lk(w_1,\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_3'))\simeq \text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_3'')\simeq\vee_3\mathbb{S}^2$ by \Cref{lemma:path_graph} and \eqref{equation:Gamma_123}.
		
		Now, assume $n\ge4$. Let $\mathcal{W}_n''': = \mathcal{W}_n''-N_{\mathcal{W}_n''}[d_2]$. Since $b_1$ is a simplicial vertex in $\mathcal{W}_n'''$ with $N_{\mathcal{W}_n'''}(b_1) = \{a_1\}$, it follows that $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_n''')\simeq \Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_n'''-N_{\mathcal{W}_n'''}[a_1]))$. Further, $\mathcal{W}_n'''-N_{\mathcal{W}_n'''}[a_1]\cong \Gamma_{n-2}$ implies $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_n''-N_{\mathcal{W}_n''}[d_2])\simeq \text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_n''')\simeq \Sigma(\text{Ind}(\Gamma_{n-2}))$.

		Observe that $N_{\mathcal{W}_n''-N_{\mathcal{W}_n''}[e_2]}(b_1)\subseteq N_{\mathcal{W}_n''-N_{\mathcal{W}_n''}[e_2]}(b_2)$. By \Cref{theorem:N(u)subsetN(v)}, $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_n''-N_{\mathcal{W}_n''}[e_2])\simeq \text{Ind}((\mathcal{W}_n''-N_{\mathcal{W}_n''}[e_2])-\{b_2\})$. Since $(\mathcal{W}_n''-N_{\mathcal{W}_n''}[e_2])-\{b_2\}\cong\mathcal{X}_{n-3}$, we have $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_n''-N_{\mathcal{W}_n''}[e_2])\simeq \text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_{n-3})$. 
		
		Therefore $lk(w_1,\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_n'))\simeq \text{Ind}( \mathcal{W}_n'')\simeq \Sigma^2 (\text{Ind}(\Gamma_{n-2}))\vee \Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_{n-3}))$ for $n\ge4$.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- claim-4: body_md -->
\label{claim:Xn_relation}
		For $n\ge3$,
		$\text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_n)\simeq\vee_2 \Sigma^2(\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n))\vee \Sigma^3 (\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_{n-1})).$
<!-- end claim-4 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
Let $\mathcal{X}_n'$ be the graph obtained from $\mathcal{X}_n$ by replacing the path $x_4x_2x_1x_3x_5$ with the edge $(x_4,x_5)$ (see \Cref{fig:Xn_2,fig:Xn'}). 
		By \Cref{theorem:replace_edge}, $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_n)\simeq \Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_n'))$. 
		Since $N_{\mathcal{X}_n'}[x_4]\subseteq N_{\mathcal{X}_n'}[x_6]$, \Cref{corollary:N[u]subsetN[v]_lk(v)_del(v)} implies $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_n')\simeq \text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_n'-\{x_6\})\vee\Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_n'-N_{\mathcal{X}_n'}[x_6]))$. 
		We have $\mathcal{X}_n'-N_{\mathcal{X}_n'}[x_6]\cong \Gamma_n$. Hence $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_n')\simeq \text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_n'-\{x_6\})\vee\Sigma(\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n))$, and therefore $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_n)\simeq \Sigma (\text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_n'-\{x_6\}))\vee\Sigma^2(\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n))$.
		\begin{figure}[H]
			\begin{subfigure}{0.30\textwidth}
				\centering
				\begin{tikzpicture}[scale = 0.27, vertices/.style = {draw, fill = black, circle, inner sep = 0.5pt}]
					
					                      
					\node[vertices,label = above:{{\tiny $x_4$}}] (x4) at (8, 4.5) {}; 
					\node[vertices,label = below:{{\tiny $x_5$}}] (x5) at (8, 1.5) {}; 
					\node[vertices,label = above:{{\tiny $x_6$}}] (x6) at (9.2, 3) {};
					\node[vertices,label = above:{{\tiny $x_7$}}] (x7) at (10.5, 4.5) {}; 
					\node[vertices,label = below:{{\tiny $x_8$}}] (x8) at (10.5, 1.5) {};
					
					\node[vertices,label = above:{{\tiny $b_1$}}] (b1) at (12, 3) {};
					\node[vertices,label = below:{{\tiny $d_1$}}] (d1) at (12, 0) {};

		Observe that $x_4$ is an isolated vertex in $(\mathcal{X}_n'-\{x_6\})-N_{\mathcal{X}_n'-\{x_6\}}[\{x_8,b_1\}]$ (see \Cref{fig:Xn'_x6}). Hence $\text{Ind}((\mathcal{X}_n'-\{x_6\})-N_{\mathcal{X}_n'-\{x_6\}}[\{x_8,b_1\}])$ is contractible. From \Cref{lemma:edge_contractible}, $ \text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_n'-\{x_6\})\simeq \text{Ind}((\mathcal{X}_n'-\{x_6\})- \{(x_8,b_1)\}).$ Let $\mathcal{X}_n'': = (\mathcal{X}_n'-\{x_6\})- \{(x_8,b_1)\}$ (see \Cref{fig:Xn''}). Then $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_n'-\{x_6\})\simeq \text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_n'')$. It follows that $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_n)\simeq \Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_n''))\vee \Sigma^2(\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n)).$
		
		Now, since $\mathcal{X}_n''-N_{\mathcal{X}_n''}[\{x_7,d_1\}]$ contains an isolated vertex $x_5$, $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_n''-N_{\mathcal{X}_n''}[\{x_7,d_1\}])$ is contractible. Hence $ \text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_n'')\simeq \text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_n''\cup \{(x_7,d_1)\})$. Moreover, in $(\mathcal{X}_n''\cup \{(x_7,d_1)\})-N_{\mathcal{X}_n''\cup \{(x_7,d_1)\}}[\{x_7,c_1\}]$, $x_5$ is an isolated vertex. Therefore $ \text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_n''\cup \{(x_7,d_1)\})\simeq \text{Ind}((\mathcal{X}_n''\cup \{(x_7,d_1)\})\cup \{(x_7,c_1)\}).$ Let $\mathcal{X}_n''': = (\mathcal{X}_n''\cup \{(x_7,d_1)\})\cup \{(x_7,c_1)\}$ (see \Cref{fig:Xn'''}). Then $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_n'')\simeq \text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_n''')$. This yields $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_n) \simeq \Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_n''')) \vee\Sigma^2(\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n)).$
		
		We have $N_{\mathcal{X}_n'''}[b_1]\subseteq N_{\mathcal{X}_n'''}[x_7]$. By \Cref{corollary:N[u]subsetN[v]_lk(v)_del(v)}, $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_n''')\simeq \text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_n'''-\{x_7\})\vee\Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_n'''-N_{\mathcal{X}_n'''}[x_7]))$. Note that $\mathcal{X}_n'''-N_{\mathcal{X}_n'''}[x_7]\cong \mathcal{U}_{n-1}\sqcup P_2$ (see \Cref{fig:Xn'''_lk_x7}). Hence $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_n''')\simeq \text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_n'''-\{x_7\})\vee\Sigma^2(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_{n-1}))$.

		Further, $x_4$ is a simplicial vertex in $\mathcal{X}_n'''-\{x_7\}$ with $N_{\mathcal{X}_n'''-\{x_7\}}[x_4] = \{x_5\}$ (see \Cref{fig:Xn'''_x7}). By \Cref{theorem:simplicial_vertex}, $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_n'''-\{x_7\})\simeq \Sigma(\text{Ind}((\mathcal{X}_n'''-\{x_7\})-N_{\mathcal{X}_n'''-\{x_7\}}[x_5]))$. Note that $(\mathcal{X}_n'''-\{x_7\})-N_{\mathcal{X}_n'''-\{x_7\}}[x_5]\cong\Gamma_n$. Hence $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_n'''-\{x_7\})\simeq \Sigma( \text{Ind}(\Gamma_n))$. 
		It follows that $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_n''')\simeq \Sigma( \text{Ind}(\Gamma_n))\vee\Sigma^2(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_{n-1}))$. Thus $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_n)\simeq \vee_2 \Sigma^2(\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n))\vee \Sigma^3 (\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_{n-1})).$

<!-- claim-5: body_md -->
\label{claim:Yn_relation}
		For $n\ge3$, $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Y}_n)\simeq \Sigma^2(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_{n-1}))\vee \Sigma^3(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_{n-2})).$
<!-- end claim-5 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
Since $y_2$ is a simplicial vertex in $\mathcal{Y}_n$ with $N_{\mathcal{Y}_n}(y_2) = \{y_6\}$ (\Cref{fig:Yn_2}), \Cref{theorem:simplicial_vertex} gives $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Y}_n)\simeq \Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Y}_n-N_{\mathcal{Y}_n}[y_6]))$. Let $\mathcal{Y}_n': = \mathcal{Y}_n-N_{\mathcal{Y}_n}[y_6]$ (see \Cref{fig:Yn'}). Then $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Y}_n)\simeq \Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Y}_n')).$

		Now, $y_1$ is a simplicial vertex in $\mathcal{Y}_n'$ with $N_{\mathcal{Y}_n'}(y_1) = \{y_3,y_5\}.$ Hence $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Y}_n')\simeq \Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Y}_n'-N_{\mathcal{Y}_n'}[y_3]))\vee \Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Y}_n'-N_{\mathcal{Y}_n'}[y_5])).$
		We have $\mathcal{Y}_n'-N_{\mathcal{Y}_n'}[y_3]\cong\mathcal{W}_{n-1}$ (see \Cref{fig:Yn'_lk_y3}). Let $\mathcal{Y}_n'': = \mathcal{Y}_n'-N_{\mathcal{Y}_n'}[y_5]$. Then $\text{Ind}( \mathcal{Y}_n')\simeq \Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_{n-1}))\vee \Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Y}_n''))$. Therefore $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Y}_n)\simeq \Sigma^2 (\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_{n-1}))\vee \Sigma^2 (\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Y}_n''))$.
		
		In $\mathcal{Y}_n''$, $y_4$ is a simplicial vertex with $N_{\mathcal{Y}_n''}(y_4) = \{a_1\}$ (see \Cref{fig:Yn''}). Hence $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Y}_n'')\simeq \Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Y}_n''-N_{\mathcal{Y}_n''}[a_1]))$. Since $\mathcal{Y}_n''-N_{\mathcal{Y}_n''}[a_1]\cong\mathcal{W}_{n-2}$, it follows that $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Y}_n)\simeq \Sigma^2(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_{n-1}))\vee \Sigma^3(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_{n-2}))$.

\label{claim:Zn_relation}
		For $n\ge3$, $lk(z_1,\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Z}_n))\simeq \text{Ind}(\mathcal{Y}_n)$ and $del(z_1,\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Z}_n))\simeq \Sigma^2 (\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_n)).$
<!-- end claim-6 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
By \eqref{equation:lk_del_ind_complex}, we have $lk(z_1,\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Z}_n))\simeq \text{Ind}(\mathcal{Z}_n-N_{\mathcal{Z}_n}[z_1])$. Since $\mathcal{Z}_n-N_{\mathcal{Z}_n}[z_1]\cong\mathcal{Y}_n$ (\Cref{fig:lk_Zn_z1}), $lk(z_1,\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Z}_n))\simeq \text{Ind}(\mathcal{Y}_n).$

		On the other hand, $del(z_1,\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Z}_n)) \simeq \text{Ind}(\mathcal{Z}_n-\{z_1\})$ by \eqref{equation:lk_del_ind_complex}. Since $z_2$ is a simplicial vertex in $\mathcal{Z}_n-\{z_1\}$ with $N_{\mathcal{Z}_n-\{z_1\}}(z_2) = \{z_4\}$ (\Cref{fig:Zn'}), \Cref{theorem:simplicial_vertex} gives $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Z}_n-\{z_1\})\simeq \Sigma(\text{Ind}((\mathcal{Z}_n-\{z_1\})-N_{\mathcal{Z}_n-\{z_1\}}[z_4])).$ Let $\mathcal{Z}_n': = (\mathcal{Z}_n-\{z_1\})-N_{\mathcal{Z}_n-\{z_1\}}[z_4]$. Then $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Z}_n-\{z_1\})\simeq \Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Z}_n'))$.
		
		Now, $z_3$ is a simplicial vertex in $\mathcal{Z}_n'$ with $N_{\mathcal{Z}_n'}(z_3) = \{z_5\}$ (see \Cref{fig:Zn''}). Hence $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Z}_n')\simeq \Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Z}_n'-N_{\mathcal{Z}_n'}[z_5]))$. Since $\mathcal{Z}_n'-N_{\mathcal{Z}_n'}[z_5]\cong\mathcal{U}_n$, we have $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Z}_n-\{z_1\})\simeq \Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Z}_n'))\simeq \Sigma^2(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_n))$. Therefore $del(z_1,\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Z}_n))\simeq \Sigma^2 (\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_n)).$

<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\label{proposition:homology_up_to_n-2} 
		Let $n\geq 1$. Then
		\begin{enumerate}[label = (\roman*)]
			\item $\tilde{H}_i(\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n)) = 0$ for all $i\le n-2$. \label{result:Gamma_n}
			\item $\tilde{H}_i(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_n)) = 0$ for all $i\le n-2$. \label{result:U_n}
			\item $\tilde{H}_i(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{V}_n)) = 0$ for all $i\le n-1$. \label{result:V_n}
			\item $\tilde{H}_i(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_n)) = 0$ for all $i\le n-1$. \label{result:W_n}
			\item $\tilde{H}_i(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_n)) = 0$ for all $i\le n$. \label{result:X_n}
			\item $\tilde{H}_i(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Y}_n)) = 0$ for all $i\le n$. \label{result:Y_n}
			\item $\tilde{H}_i(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Z}_n)) = 0$ for all $i\le n$. \label{result:Z_n}
		\end{enumerate}
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
The proof is by induction on $n$. We consider the base cases $n = 1,2.$ For these cases, the required results follow directly from the homotopy types given in \eqref{equation:Gamma_123}--\eqref{equation:Z_12} and the fact that $\tilde{H}_{-1}(\Delta) = 0$ whenever $\Delta\neq \emptyset$. Let $n\geq 3,$ and assume that the result holds for all $1\leq l\leq n-1.$ We now prove the results for $n$.
		\begin{enumerate}[label = (\roman*)]
			\item $\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n)$. To analyze $\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n)$, we consider the link and deletion of the vertex $b_1$ in $\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n)$. We have $lk(b_1, \text{Ind}(\Gamma_n))\simeq \text{Ind}(\Gamma_n-N_{\Gamma_n}[b_1])$ and $del(b_1, \text{Ind}(\Gamma_n))\simeq \text{Ind}(\Gamma_n-\{b_1\})$ by \eqref{equation:lk_del_ind_complex}. 
			Observe that $\Gamma_n-N_{\Gamma_n}[b_1]\cong\mathcal{U}_{n-1}$ and $\Gamma_n-\{b_1\}\cong\mathcal{V}_{n-1}$. Hence $lk(b_1, \text{Ind}(\Gamma_n))\simeq \text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_{n-1})$ and $del(b_1, \text{Ind}(\Gamma_n))\simeq \text{Ind}(\mathcal{V}_{n-1})$.
			Substituting $lk(b_1, \text{Ind}(\Gamma_n))$ and $del(b_1, \text{Ind}(\Gamma_n))$ into the Mayer-Vietoris sequence given in \eqref{equation:MayerVietoris}, we have
			\begin{align*}
				\cdots \to& \tilde{H}_{n-1}(\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n))\to \tilde{H}_{n-2}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_{n-1}))\to \tilde{H}_{n-2}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{V}_{n-1}))\to \tilde{H}_{n-2}(\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n))\to\\
				& \tilde{H}_{n-3}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_{n-1}))\to \tilde{H}_{n-3}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{V}_{n-1}))\to \tilde{H}_{n-3}(\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n))\to \tilde{H}_{n-4}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_{n-1}))\to\cdots
			\end{align*}
			By the induction hypothesis, $\tilde{H}_i(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_{n-1})) = 0$ for all $i\le n-3$ and $\tilde{H}_i(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{V}_{n-1})) = 0$ for all $i\le n-2$. This gives
			\begin{align*}
				\cdots \to & \tilde{H}_{n-1}(\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n))\to \tilde{H}_{n-2}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_{n-1}))\to 0\to \\
				&\tilde{H}_{n-2}(\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n))\to 0 \to 0 \to \tilde{H}_{n-3}(\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n))\to 0 \to\cdots
			\end{align*}
			From the exactness of the above sequence, $\tilde{H}_i(\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n)) = 0$ for all $i\le n-2$.
			
			\item $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_n).$ For $n = 3$, the result follows from \Cref{claim:Un_relation}. Now assume that $n \geq 4$. By \Cref{claim:Un_relation},
			$\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_n)\simeq \Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_{n-1}))\vee\Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Z}_{n-3}))\vee\Sigma^3 (\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_{n-3})).$ It follows that $\tilde{H}_i(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_n)) \cong \tilde{H}_{i-1}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_{n-1}))\oplus\tilde{H}_{i-1}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Z}_{n-3}))\oplus\tilde{H}_{i-3}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_{n-3}))$. 
			By the induction hypothesis, we have $\tilde{H}_{i-1}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_{n-1})) = 0$, $\tilde{H}_{i-1}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Z}_{n-3})) = 0$ and $\tilde{H}_{i-3}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_{n-3})) = 0$ for all $i\leq n-2$. Therefore $\tilde{H}_i(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_n)) = 0$ for all $i\leq n-2$.
			
			\item $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{V}_n)$. Since $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{V}_n)\simeq \Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_{n-2}))\vee \Sigma^2 (\text{Ind}(\Gamma_{n-1}))$ by \Cref{claim:Vn_relation}, we have $\tilde{H}_i(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{V}_n))\cong \tilde{H}_{i-1}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_{n-2}))\oplus \tilde{H}_{i-2}(\text{Ind}(\Gamma_{n-1})).$ 
			For $i\leq n-1$, $\tilde{H}_{i-1}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_{n-2})) = 0$ and $\tilde{H}_{i-2}(\text{Ind}(\Gamma_{n-1})) = 0$ by the induction hypothesis. Hence $\tilde{H}_i(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{V}_n)) = 0$ for all $i\leq n-1$.
			
			\item $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_n)$. By \Cref{claim:Wn_relation}, $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_n)\simeq \text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_n')$, where $\mathcal{W}_n' = \mathcal{W}_n-\{d_1\}$. We consider the link and deletion of the vertex $w_1$ in $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_n')$. For $n = 3$, \Cref{claim:Wn_relation} gives $lk(w_1,\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_3'))\simeq \vee_3\mathbb{S}^2$ and $del(w_1,\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_3'))\simeq \text{Ind}(\mathcal{Y}_{2})$. Using the Mayer-Vietoris sequence given in \eqref{equation:MayerVietoris}, we obtain
			\begin{align*}
				\cdots\to &\tilde{H}_{3}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_3'))\to \tilde{H}_{2}(\vee_3\mathbb{S}^2)\to \tilde{H}_{2}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Y}_{2})) \to \tilde{H}_{2}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_3'))
				\to \\&\tilde{H}_{1}(\vee_3\mathbb{S}^2)\to \tilde{H}_{1}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Y}_{2})) \to \tilde{H}_{1}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_3'))\to \tilde{H}_{0}(\vee_3\mathbb{S}^2)\to\cdots 
			\end{align*}
			Since $\tilde{H}_2(\vee_3\mathbb{S}^2) \cong\mathbb{Z}^3$ and $\tilde{H}_i(\vee_3\mathbb{S}^2) = 0$ for all $i\ne2$, and $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Y}_{2})\simeq\text{pt}$ by \eqref{equation:Y_12}, it follows that 
			\begin{equation*}
				\cdots\to \tilde{H}_{3}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_3'))\to \mathbb{Z}^3\to 0 \to \tilde{H}_{2}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_3'))\to 0 \to 0 \to \tilde{H}_{1}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_3'))\to 0 \to\cdots
			\end{equation*}
			The exactness of the above sequence yields $\tilde{H}_i(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_3')) = 0$ for all $i\le 2$. Since $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_3)\simeq \text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_3')$, we conclude that $\tilde{H}_i(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_3)) = 0$ for all $i\le 2$.
			
			Now let $n\geq 4$. By \Cref{claim:Wn_relation}, we have $lk(w_1, \text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_n'))\simeq \Sigma^2 (\text{Ind}(\Gamma_{n-2}))\vee \Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_{n-3}))$ and $del(w_1, \text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_n'))\simeq \text{Ind}(\mathcal{Y}_{n-1})$.
			Substituting these into the Mayer-Vietoris sequence \eqref{equation:MayerVietoris}, we obtain 
			\begin{align*}
				\cdots \to & \tilde{H}_n(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_n'))\to \tilde{H}_{n-1}(\Sigma^2 (\text{Ind}(\Gamma_{n-2}))\vee \Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_{n-3})))\to \tilde{H}_{n-1}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Y}_{n-1}))\to\\
				& \tilde{H}_{n-1}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_n'))\to \tilde{H}_{n-2}(\Sigma^2 (\text{Ind}(\Gamma_{n-2}))\vee \Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_{n-3})))\to \tilde{H}_{n-2}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Y}_{n-1})) \to\\
				& \tilde{H}_{n-2}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_n'))\to \tilde{H}_{n-3}(\Sigma^2 (\text{Ind}(\Gamma_{n-2}))\vee \Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_{n-3})))\to\cdots
			\end{align*}
			This implies
			\begin{align*}
				\cdots \to & \tilde{H}_n(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_n'))\to \tilde{H}_{n-3}(\text{Ind}(\Gamma_{n-2}))\oplus \tilde{H}_{n-2}( \text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_{n-3})) \to \tilde{H}_{n-1}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Y}_{n-1}))\to\\
				& \tilde{H}_{n-1}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_n'))\to \tilde{H}_{n-4}(\text{Ind}(\Gamma_{n-2}))\oplus \tilde{H}_{n-3}( \text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_{n-3})) \to \tilde{H}_{n-2}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Y}_{n-1}))\to\\
				& \tilde{H}_{n-2}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_n'))\to \tilde{H}_{n-5}(\text{Ind}(\Gamma_{n-2}))\oplus \tilde{H}_{n-4}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_{n-3}))\to\cdots
			\end{align*}
			By the induction hypothesis, $\tilde{H}_i(\text{Ind}(\Gamma_{n-2})) = 0$ for all $i\le n-4$, $\tilde{H}_i(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_{n-3})) = 0$ for all $i\le n-3$, and $\tilde{H}_i(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Y}_{n-1})) = 0$ for all $i\le n-1$. Hence
			\begin{align*}
				\cdots \to & \tilde{H}_n(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_n'))\to \tilde{H}_{n-3}(\text{Ind}(\Gamma_{n-2}))\oplus \tilde{H}_{n-2}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_{n-3}))\to 0 \to\\
				& \tilde{H}_{n-1}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_n'))\to 0 \to 0 \to \tilde{H}_{n-2}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_n'))\to 0 \to\cdots
			\end{align*}
			Using the exactness of the above sequence, $\tilde{H}_i(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_n')) = 0$ for all $i\le n-1$. Since $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_n)\simeq \text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_n')$, it follows that $\tilde{H}_i(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_n)) = 0$ for all $i\le n-1$.
			
			\item $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_n)$. By \Cref{claim:Xn_relation}, $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_n)\simeq \vee_2 \Sigma^2(\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n))\vee \Sigma^3 (\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_{n-1})).$ This implies $\tilde{H}_i(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_n))\cong\tilde{H}_{i-2}(\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n))\oplus \tilde{H}_{i-2}(\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n))\oplus\tilde{H}_{i-3}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_{n-1})).$
			For $i\le n$, we have $\tilde{H}_{i-2}(\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n)) = 0$ by \ref{result:Gamma_n}, and $\tilde{H}_{i-3}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_{n-1})) = 0$ by the induction hypothesis. Thus $\tilde{H}_i(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{X}_n)) = 0$ for all $i\leq n$.
			
			\item $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Y}_n)$. Since $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Y}_n)\simeq\Sigma^2 (\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_{n-1}))\vee \Sigma^3 (\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_{n-2}))$ by \Cref{claim:Yn_relation}, it follows that $\tilde{H}_i(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Y}_n))\cong \tilde{H}_{i-2}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_{n-1}))\oplus\tilde{H}_{i-3}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_{n-2}))$. 
			For $i\le n$, the induction hypothesis gives $\tilde{H}_{i-3}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_{n-2})) = 0$ and $\tilde{H}_{i-2}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{W}_{n-1})) = 0$.  Therefore $\tilde{H}_i(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Y}_n)) = 0$ for all $i\leq n$.
			
			\item $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Z}_n)$. By \Cref{claim:Zn_relation}, $lk(z_1, \text{Ind}(\mathcal{Z}_n))\simeq \text{Ind}(\mathcal{Y}_n)$ and $del(z_1, \text{Ind}(\mathcal{Z}_n))\simeq \Sigma^2 (\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_n))$. Substituting these into the Mayer-Vietoris sequence \eqref{equation:MayerVietoris}, we obtain
			\begin{align*}
				\cdots \to & \tilde{H}_{n+1}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Z}_n)) \to \tilde{H}_n(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Y}_n))\to \tilde{H}_n(\Sigma^2 (\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_n)))\to \tilde{H}_n(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Z}_n)) \to\\
				& \tilde{H}_{n-1}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Y}_n)) \to \tilde{H}_{n-1}(\Sigma^2 (\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_n)))\to \tilde{H}_{n-1}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Z}_n))\to \tilde{H}_{n-2}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Y}_n))\to\cdots
			\end{align*}
			This implies
			\begin{align*}
				\cdots \to & \tilde{H}_{n+1}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Z}_n)) \to \tilde{H}_n(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Y}_n))\to \tilde{H}_{n-2}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_n))\to \tilde{H}_n(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Z}_n))\to\\
				& \tilde{H}_{n-1}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Y}_n)) \to \tilde{H}_{n-3}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_n))\to \tilde{H}_{n-1}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Z}_n))\to \tilde{H}_{n-2}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Y}_n))\to\cdots
			\end{align*} 
			Since $\tilde{H}_i(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Y}_n)) = 0$ for all $i\le n$ by \ref{result:Y_n} and $\tilde{H}_i(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_n)) = 0$ for all $i\le n-2$ by \ref{result:U_n}, we have
			\begin{align*}
				\cdots & \to \tilde{H}_{n+1}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Z}_n)) \to 0 \to 0 \to \tilde{H}_n(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Z}_n))\to 0 \to 0\to \tilde{H}_{n-1}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Z}_n))\to 0 \to\cdots
			\end{align*}
			The exactness of the above sequence implies $\tilde{H}_i(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Z}_n)) = 0$ for all $i\le n$.
		\end{enumerate}
		This completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
In the proof of \Cref{proposition:homology_up_to_n-2}, the idea of considering auxiliary graphs (as defined in \Cref{subsection:graph_definitions}) and establishing recursive relations among the homology groups of independence complexes of these graphs and of $\Gamma_n$, in terms of link and deletion, is taken from \cite{Previous_3Xn}.
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label{proposition:homology_n-1} 
		For $n\geq1,$ $\tilde{H}_{n-1}(\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n)) \neq 0 \text{ and }\tilde{H}_{n-1}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_n)) \neq 0$.
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
We proceed by induction on $n$. The results for the base cases $n = 1,2,3$ are immediate from \eqref{equation:Gamma_123}, \eqref{equation:U_12} and \Cref{claim:Un_relation}. 
		
		Let $n>3$ and assume that the results hold for all $1\leq l\leq n-1$. We now prove the results for $n.$
		
		For $\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n)$, we consider the link and deletion of the vertex $b_1$ in $\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n)$. Since $lk(b_1, \text{Ind}(\Gamma_n))\simeq \text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_{n-1})$ and $del(b_1, \text{Ind}(\Gamma_n))\simeq \text{Ind}(\mathcal{V}_{n-1})$, using the Mayer-Vietoris sequence \eqref{equation:MayerVietoris}, we obtain
		\begin{align*}
			\cdots & \to \tilde{H}_{n-1}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{V}_{n-1}))\to \tilde{H}_{n-1}(\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n))\to \tilde{H}_{n-2}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_{n-1}))\to \tilde{H}_{n-2}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{V}_{n-1}))\to \ldots
		\end{align*}
		We have $\tilde{H}_{n-2}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_{n-1}))\ne0$ by the induction hypothesis, and $\tilde{H}_{n-2}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{V}_{n-1})) = 0$ by \Cref{proposition:homology_up_to_n-2}. Therefore, the exactness of the above sequence implies that $ \tilde{H}_{n-1}(\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n)) \neq 0$.
		
		For $\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_n)$, we have
		$\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_n)\simeq \Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_{n-1}))\vee\Sigma(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Z}_{n-3}))\vee\Sigma^3 (\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_{n-3}))$ by \Cref{claim:Un_relation}. 
		It follows that $\tilde{H}_{n-1}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_n))\cong \tilde{H}_{n-2}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_{n-1}))\oplus\tilde{H}_{n-2}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{Z}_{n-3}))\oplus\tilde{H}_{n-4}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_{n-3}))$. By the induction hypothesis, $\tilde{H}_{n-2}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_{n-1}))\ne0$. Hence $\tilde{H}_{n-1}(\text{Ind}(\mathcal{U}_n))\neq 0$.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- definition-4: body_md -->
A \textit{matching} on $\mathcal{F}$ is a family $\mathcal{M}$ of pairs $\{\sigma,\tau\}$ with $\sigma,\tau\in\mathcal{F}$ such that each element of $\mathcal{F}$ belongs to at most one pair of $\mathcal{M}$.
<!-- end definition-4 -->
<!-- definition-5: body_md -->
A matching $\mathcal{M}$ on $\mathcal{F}$ is called a \textit{partial matching} if, for every pair $\{\sigma,\tau\}\in\mathcal{M}$, either $\sigma\prec\tau$ (\textit{i.e.}, $\sigma\subset\tau$ and no $\rho\in\mathcal{F}$ satisfies $ \sigma\subset\rho\subset\tau$), or $\sigma\succ \tau$.
<!-- end definition-5 -->
<!-- definition-6: body_md -->
A partial matching $\mathcal{M}$ on $\mathcal{F}$ is called {\it acyclic} if there does not exist a cycle
		$$\phi(\sigma_1) \succ \sigma_1 \prec \phi(\sigma_2) \succ \sigma_2 \prec \phi(\sigma_3) \succ \sigma_3 \prec \dots \prec \phi(\sigma_r) \succ \sigma_r \prec \phi(\sigma_1),$$
		with $\ r\ge 2$, and all $\sigma_i\in\mathcal{F}$ being distinct.
<!-- end definition-6 -->
<!-- definition-7: body_md -->
Let $a\in A$. A matching $\mathcal{M}_a$ on $\mathcal{F}$ is called an \textit{element matching} using $a$ if every pair in $\mathcal{M}_a$ is of the form $\{\sigma\setminus\{a\},\sigma\cup\{a\}\}$ for some $\sigma\in\mathcal{F}$.
<!-- end definition-7 -->
<!-- lemma-9: body_md -->
\label{lemma:Jonsson acyclic}
		Let $a\in A$. Define $$\mathcal{M}_a: = \{\{\sigma\setminus\{a\},\sigma\cup\{a\}\}\, |\, \sigma\setminus\{a\},\, \sigma\cup\{a\}\in\mathcal{F}\}.$$ 
		Let $\mathcal{M}'$ be an acyclic matching on $\mathcal{F}': = \{\sigma\in\mathcal{F}\, |\, \sigma\notin\mathcal{M}_a\}$. Then $\mathcal{M}: = \mathcal{M}_a \cup\mathcal{M}'$ is an acyclic matching on $\mathcal{F}$.
<!-- end lemma-9 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
\label{theorem:acyclic}
		Let $\Delta$ be a simplicial complex and let $\mathcal{M}$ be an acyclic matching on $\Delta$ such that the empty set is not critical. Then $\Delta$ is homotopy equivalent to a cell complex with one cell of dimension $d \ge 0$ for each critical face of $\Delta$ of dimension $d$ plus one additional $0$-cell.
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
\label{remark:al_G_and_sigma_union_v_notin_Ind(G)}
		Let $\sigma\in\text{Ind}(G)$. Then $\sigma\in\mathcal{I}_{G}$ if and only if $|\sigma| = \alpha({G})$. Consequently, if $\sigma\in\mathcal{I}_{G}$ and $v\in V(G)\setminus\sigma$, then $\sigma\cup\{v\}\notin\text{Ind}(G)$.
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- lemma-10: body_md -->
\label{lemma:matching_top_dimension_in_G}
		Let $\mathcal{S} = \{v_1, v_2,v_3,\cdots,v_t\}\subseteq V(G)$ be an ordered independent set in $G$. For $\sigma\in \mathcal{I}_{G}$, $\sigma\in \mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}$ if and only if $\sigma\cap\mathcal{S}\ne\emptyset$.
<!-- end lemma-10 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
Assume first that $\sigma \in \mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}$. Since $\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}} = \bigsqcup_{i = 1}^{t}\mathcal{M}_{v_i}$, there exists $1\le j\le t$ such that $\sigma\in \mathcal{M}_{v_j}$. Suppose $v_j\notin \sigma$. Then $\sigma = \sigma\setminus\{v_j\}$ is matched with $\sigma\cup\{v_j\}$ in $\mathcal{M}_{v_j}$. It follows that $\sigma\cup \{v_j\}\in \text{Ind}(G)$, contradicting \Cref{lemma:matching_top_dimension_in_G} (as $\sigma\in \mathcal{I}_{G}$). Therefore $v_j\in \sigma$, and hence $\sigma\cap\mathcal{S}\ne\emptyset$.
		
		We now assume that $\sigma\cap\mathcal{S}\ne\emptyset$. Let $j: = \min\{i: v_i\in \sigma\}$. We prove that $\sigma\in\mathcal{M}_{v_j}$ and hence $\sigma\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}$. On the contrary, suppose $\sigma\notin \mathcal{M}_{v_j}$. Since $v_j\in\sigma$, it follows that $\sigma\cup\{v_j\} = \sigma\in\mathcal{M}_{v_{j'}}$ or $\sigma\setminus\{v_j\}\in \mathcal{M}_{v_{j''}}$ for some $j',j''<j$. By the minimality of $j$, we have $v_{j'}\notin\sigma$ and $v_{j''}\notin\sigma\setminus\{v_j\}$.
		
		If $\sigma\in\mathcal{M}_{v_{j'}}$, then since $\sigma = \sigma\setminus\{v_{j'}\}$ can only be matched with $\sigma\cup\{v_{j'}\}$ in $\mathcal{M}_{v_{j'}}$, we have $\sigma\cup\{v_{j'}\}\in \text{Ind}(G)$. This contradicts \Cref{remark:al_G_and_sigma_union_v_notin_Ind(G)}. Hence $\sigma\setminus\{v_j\}\in \mathcal{M}_{v_{j''}}$. Now, since $\sigma\setminus\{v_j\} = \sigma\setminus\{v_{j},v_{j''}\}$ can only be matched with $(\sigma\setminus\{v_j\})\cup\{v_{j''}\}$ in $\mathcal{M}_{v_{j''}}$, we get $(\sigma\setminus\{v_j\})\cup\{v_{j''}\}\in \text{Ind}(G)$. 
		This implies $v_{j''}\nsim v$ for all $v\in\sigma\setminus\{v_j\}$. Since $\mathcal{S}$ is an independent set, $v_{j''}\nsim v_j$. It follows that $\sigma\cup\{v_{j''}\}\in \text{Ind}(G)$, again a contradiction to \Cref{remark:al_G_and_sigma_union_v_notin_Ind(G)}. Thus $\sigma\in\mathcal{M}_{v_j}$, which implies $\sigma\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}$.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- lemma-11: body_md -->
\label{lemma:dim_sigma}
		Let $\sigma\in\mathcal{I}_{\Gamma_n}$. Then 
		$\alpha({\Gamma_n}) = |\sigma| 
		= \begin{cases}
			\frac{3n}{2} & \text{if $n$ is even,}\\
			\frac{3n-1}{2} & \text{if $n$ is odd}.
		\end{cases}$
<!-- end lemma-11 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
By \Cref{remark:al_G_and_sigma_union_v_notin_Ind(G)}, $\alpha({\Gamma_n}) = |\sigma|$. We now compute $|\sigma|$.
		
		Since $\sigma \in \mathcal{I}_{\Gamma_n},$ $|\sigma| = \dim(\text{Ind}(\Gamma_{ n}))+1$. Using $\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n) = M(G_{3\times n}),$ we obtain $|\sigma| = \dim(M(G_{3\times n}))+1$. By the definition of the matching complex, $|\sigma|$ equals the size of a maximum matching in $G_{3\times n}$.
		
		We first assume that $n$ is even. Then $M = \{(11,21),(12,22),(13,23),(31,41),(32,42),(33,43),\ldots,((n-1)1,n1),((n-1)2,n2),((n-1)3,n3)\}$ is a matching of size $3n/2$ that covers the entire vertex set $V(G_{3\times n})$. Therefore $M$ is a maximum matching, and hence
		$|\sigma| = \frac{3n}{2}$.
		
		Now, suppose that $n$ is odd. Since $|V(G_{3\times n})| = 3n$ is odd, every matching in $G_{3\times n}$ leaves at least one vertex uncovered. Observe that $M' = \{(11,21),(12,22),(13,23),(31,41),(32,42),(33,43),\ldots,((n-2)1,(n-1)1),((n-2)2,(n-1)2),((n-2)3,(n-1)3),(n1,n2)\}$ is a matching of size $\frac{3n-1}{2}$ that covers every vertex of $G_{3\times n}$ except $n3$. Thus $M'$ is a maximum matching, and therefore $|\sigma| = \frac{3n-1}{2}$.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- lemma-12: body_md -->
\label{lemma:3m+1}
		For $m\ge 1,$ let $\sigma\in \mathcal{I}_{\Gamma_{2m+1}}$. Then $\sigma\cap\{ b_1, c_1, d_1\}\ne\emptyset$.
<!-- end lemma-12 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
The proof proceeds by induction on $m$. For the base case $m = 1$, we have
		\begin{align*}
			\mathcal{I}_{\Gamma_3}& = \{\{a_1, c_1, e_1, b_3\}, \{a_1, c_1, e_1, d_3\}, \{a_1, c_1, e_2, b_3\}, \{a_1, d_1, c_2, e_2\}, \{a_1, d_1, d_2, b_3\}, \{a_1, d_1, d_2, d_3\},\\
			&\qquad \{a_1, d_1, e_2, b_3\}, \{b_1, e_1, a_2, c_2\}, \{b_1, e_1, a_2, d_3\}, \{b_1, e_1, b_2, b_3\}, \{b_1, e_1, b_2, d_3\}, \{b_1, a_2, c_2, e_2\},\\
			&\qquad \{b_1, a_2, d_2, d_3\}, \{b_1, b_2, e_2, b_3\}, \{c_1, e_1, a_2, d_3\}, \{d_1, a_2, c_2, e_2\}, \{d_1, a_2, d_2, d_3\}, \{d_1, b_2, e_2, b_3\}\}.
		\end{align*}
		Clearly, every $\sigma\in \mathcal{I}_{\Gamma_3}$ satisfies $\sigma\cap\{ b_1, c_1, d_1\}\ne\emptyset$. Hence the result is true for $m = 1$.
		
		Let $m>1$ and assume that the result holds for all $1\le l\le m-1$. We now prove it for $m$. 
		
		Let $\sigma\in \mathcal{I}_{\Gamma_{2m+1}}$. Since $\Gamma_3$ is a subgraph of $\Gamma_{2m+1}$, we define $\Omega: = \Gamma_3-\{b_3,d_3\}$ and $\Omega': = \Gamma_{2m+1}- V(\Omega)$. Then $V(\Gamma_{2m+1}) = V(\Omega)\sqcup V(\Omega')$. It follows that $\sigma = \sigma_1\sqcup \sigma_2$ for some $\sigma_1\in \text{Ind}(\Omega)$ and $\sigma_2\in \text{Ind}(\Omega')$. Observe that $$\mathcal{I}_{\Omega} = \{\{a_1, d_1, c_2, e_2\}, \{b_1, e_1, a_2, c_2\}, \{b_1, a_2, c_2, e_2\}, \{d_1, a_2, c_2, e_2\}\}.$$ Since every element of $\mathcal{I}_{\Omega}$ has cardinality $4$, \Cref{remark:al_G_and_sigma_union_v_notin_Ind(G)} implies $\alpha({\Omega}) = 4$. Hence $|\sigma_1|\le4$. 
		\begin{figure}[H]
			\centering
			\begin{tikzpicture}[scale = 0.3, vertices/.style = {draw, fill = black, circle, inner sep = 0.5pt}]
				
				                      
				\node[vertices,label = above:{{\tiny $b_1$}}] (b1) at (12, 3) {};
				\node[vertices,label = below:{{\tiny $d_1$}}] (d1) at (12, 0) {};
				
				\node[vertices,label = above:{{\tiny $a_1$}}] (a1) at (13.5, 4.5) {};
				\node[vertices,label = below:{{\tiny $c_1$}}] (c1) at (13.5, 1.5) {};
				\node[vertices,label = below:{{\tiny $e_1$}}] (e1) at (13.5, -1.5) {};
				
				\node[vertices,label = above:{{\tiny $b_2$}}] (b2) at (15, 3) {};
				\node[vertices,label = below:{{\tiny $d_2$}}] (d2) at (15, 0) {};
				
				\node[vertices,label = above:{{\tiny $a_2$}}] (a2) at (16.5, 4.5) {};
				\node[vertices,label = below:{{\tiny $c_2$}}] (c2) at (16.5, 1.5) {};
				\node[vertices,label = below:{{\tiny $e_2$}}] (e2) at (16.5, -1.5) {};
				
				\node[vertices,label = above:{{\tiny $b_3$}}] (b3) at (18, 3) {};
				\node[vertices,label = below:{{\tiny $d_3$}}] (d3) at (18, 0) {};
				
				\node[vertices,label = above:{{\tiny $a_3$}}] (a) at (19.5, 4.5) {};
				\node[vertices,label = below:{{\tiny $c_3$}}] (c) at (19.5, 1.5) {};
				\node[vertices,label = below:{{\tiny $e_3$}}] (e) at (19.5, -1.5) {};
				
				\node[vertices,label = above:{{\tiny $b_4$}}] (b) at (21, 3) {};
				\node[vertices,label = below:{{\tiny $d_4$}}] (d) at (21, 0) {};
				
				\node[vertices, label = {[xshift = .08cm,yshift = -.01cm]above:{{{\tiny $b_{2m}$}}}}] (1) at ($(b)+(4,0)$) {};
				\node[vertices, label = {[xshift = .05cm]below:{{{\tiny $d_{2m}$}}}}] (2) at ($(d)+(4,0)$) {};
				
				\node[vertices,label = {[xshift = .1cm]above:{{{\tiny $a_{2m}$}}}}] (3) at ($(a)+(7,0)$) {}; 
				\node[vertices,label = {[xshift = .1cm]below:{{{\tiny $c_{2m}$}}}}] (4) at ($(c)+(7,0)$) {};
				\node[vertices,label = {[xshift = .1cm]below:{{{\tiny $e_{2m}$}}}}] (5) at ($(e)+(7,0)$) {};
				
				\node[vertices,label = {[xshift = .18cm]above:{{{\tiny $b_{2m+1}$}}}}] (6) at ($(b)+(7,0)$) {};
				\node[vertices,label = {[xshift = .19cm]below:{{{\tiny $d_{2m+1}$}}}}] (7) at ($(d)+(7,0)$) {};
				
				                         
				\draw (a) -- ++(2.3,0);
				\draw (c) -- ++(2.3,0);
				\draw (e) -- ++(2.3,0);
				\draw (3) -- ++(-2.3,0);
				\draw (4) -- ++(-2.3,0);
				\draw (5) -- ++(-2.3,0);
				\draw (b) -- ++(1,1);
				\draw (b) -- ++(1,-1);
				\draw (d) -- ++(1,1);
				\draw (d) -- ++(1,-1);
				\draw (1) -- ++(-1,1);
				\draw (1) -- ++(-1,-1);
				\draw (2) -- ++(-1,1);
				\draw (2) -- ++(-1,-1);
				
				                  
				\node at ($(b)!0.5!(1)$) {$\ldots$};
				\node at ($(d)!0.5!(2)$) {$\ldots$};
				\node at ($(a)!0.5!(3)$) {$\ldots$};
				\node at ($(c)!0.5!(4)$) {$\ldots$};
				\node at ($(e)!0.5!(5)$) {$\ldots$};
				
				                         
				\foreach \vertex/\neighbors in {
					b1/{a1,c1,d1},
					d1/{c1,e1},
					a1/{a2,b2},
					c1/{b2,c2,d2},
					e1/{d2,e2},
					b2/{a2,c2,d2},
					d2/{c2,e2}}
				{
					\foreach \neighbor in \neighbors
					\draw [blue] (\vertex)--(\neighbor);
				}
				
				                        
				\foreach \vertex/\neighbors in {
					a2/{a,b3},
					c2/{b3,c,d3},
					e2/{d3,e}}
				{
					\foreach \neighbor in \neighbors
					\draw [red] (\vertex)--(\neighbor);
				}
				
				            
				\foreach \vertex/\neighbors in {
					b3/{d3},
					a/{b3,b},
					b/{c,d},
					c/{b3,d3,d},
					d/{e},
					e/{d3},
					1/{2,3,4},
					2/{4,5},
					3/{6},
					4/{6,7},
					5/{7},
					6/{7}}
				{
					\foreach \neighbor in \neighbors
					\draw [-] (\vertex)--(\neighbor);
				} 
			\end{tikzpicture}
			\vspace{-.2cm}
			\caption{$\Gamma_{2m+1}$ with subgraphs $\Omega$ (blue edges) and $\Omega'$ (black edges)}
			\label{fig:Gamma_2m+1}
		\end{figure}
		Further, by \Cref{lemma:dim_sigma}, $\alpha({\Gamma_{2m-1}}) = 3m-2$. Since $\Omega'\cong \Gamma_{2m-1}$, we have $|\sigma_2|\le \alpha({\Omega'}) = \alpha({\Gamma_{2m-1}}) = 3m-2$. Moreover, $\sigma\in\mathcal{I}_{\Gamma_{2m+1}}$ implies $|\sigma| = 3m+1$ by \Cref{lemma:dim_sigma}. Therefore, there are two possible cases:
		\begin{enumerate}[label = (\roman*)]
			\item $|\sigma_1| = 3$ and $|\sigma_2| = 3m-2$.\\ 
			Then $|\sigma_2| = \alpha({\Omega'})$, and hence \Cref{remark:al_G_and_sigma_union_v_notin_Ind(G)} implies $\sigma_2\in \mathcal{I}_{\Omega'}$. Using the fact that $\Omega'\cong \Gamma_{2m-1}$, we may apply the induction hypothesis to conclude $\sigma_2 \cap\{b_3,c_3,d_3\}\ne \emptyset$. Since $c_2\sim v$ for all $v\in\{b_3,c_3,d_3\}$ in $\Gamma_{2m+1}$, it follows that $c_2\notin \sigma_1$. Let $\Omega'': = \Omega-\{b_1,c_1,d_1,c_2\}$. If $\sigma_1\cap\{b_1,c_1,d_1\} = \emptyset,$ then $\sigma_1\in \text{Ind}(\Omega'')$ (as $\sigma_1 \in \text{Ind}(\Omega)$). Observe that $\alpha({\Omega''}) = 2$, which implies $|\sigma_1|\le\alpha({\Omega''}) = 2$, a contradiction. Hence $\sigma_1\cap\{b_1, c_1, d_1\}\ne \emptyset$, and thus $\sigma\cap\{b_1,c_1,d_1\}\ne\emptyset$. 
			
			\item $|\sigma_1| = 4$ and $|\sigma_2| = 3m-3$.\\ 
			In this case, $\sigma_1 \in \mathcal{I}_{\Omega}$ (as $|\sigma_1| = \alpha({\Omega})$). Since every $\sigma''\in \mathcal{I}_{ \Omega}$ satisfies $\sigma''\cap\{b_1,c_1,d_1\}\ne \emptyset$, we get $\sigma_1\cap\{b_1,c_1,d_1\}\ne \emptyset$. Hence $\sigma\cap\{b_1, c_1, d_1\}\ne \emptyset$. 
		\end{enumerate}
		In either case, $\sigma\cap\{b_1, c_1, d_1\}\ne \emptyset$. This completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- lemma-13: body_md -->
\label{prop:even+odd}
		For $n\ge 4$, let $\sigma\in \mathcal{I}_{\Gamma_{n}}$. Then $\sigma\cap \mathcal{B}_{n}\ne \emptyset$
<!-- end lemma-13 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
We consider the cases of $n$ even and $n$ odd separately. 
		
		\noindent \textbf{Case 1:} $n = 2m$ for some integer $m\geq 2.$ 
		
		We have $\sigma\in \mathcal{I}_{\Gamma_{2m}}$. Let $\Omega: = \Gamma_2-\{b_2,d_2\}$ and $\Omega': = \Gamma_{2m}- V(\Omega)$ (as $\Gamma_2$ is a subgraph of $\Gamma_{2m}$). Then $V(\Gamma_{2m}) = V(\Omega)\sqcup V(\Omega')$. This gives $\sigma = \sigma_1\sqcup \sigma_2$ for some $\sigma_1\in \text{Ind}(\Omega)$ and $\sigma_2\in \text{Ind}(\Omega')$. It is easy to verify that $\mathcal{I}_{\Omega} = \{\{a_1,c_1,e_1\}\}$, and hence $\alpha({\Omega}) = 3$ by \Cref{remark:al_G_and_sigma_union_v_notin_Ind(G)}. Thus $|\sigma_1|\le 3$.
		\begin{figure}[H]
			\centering
			\begin{tikzpicture}[scale = 0.3, vertices/.style = {draw, fill = black, circle, inner sep = 0.5pt}]
				
				                      
				\node[vertices,label = above:{{\tiny $b_1$}}] (b1) at (12, 3) {};
				\node[vertices,label = below:{{\tiny $d_1$}}] (d1) at (12, 0) {};
				
				\node[vertices,label = above:{{\tiny $a_1$}}] (a1) at (13.5, 4.5) {};
				\node[vertices,label = below:{{\tiny $c_1$}}] (c1) at (13.5, 1.5) {};
				\node[vertices,label = below:{{\tiny $e_1$}}] (e1) at (13.5, -1.5) {};
				
				\node[vertices,label = above:{{\tiny $b_2$}}] (b2) at (15, 3) {};
				\node[vertices,label = below:{{\tiny $d_2$}}] (d2) at (15, 0) {};
				
				\node[vertices,label = above:{{\tiny $a_2$}}] (a2) at (16.5, 4.5) {};
				\node[vertices,label = below:{{\tiny $c_2$}}] (c2) at (16.5, 1.5) {};
				\node[vertices,label = below:{{\tiny $e_2$}}] (e2) at (16.5, -1.5) {};
				
				\node[vertices,label = above:{{\tiny $b_3$}}] (b3) at (18, 3) {};
				\node[vertices,label = below:{{\tiny $d_3$}}] (d3) at (18, 0) {};
				
				\node[vertices,label = above:{{\tiny $a_3$}}] (a) at (19.5, 4.5) {};
				\node[vertices,label = below:{{\tiny $c_3$}}] (c) at (19.5, 1.5) {};
				\node[vertices,label = below:{{\tiny $e_3$}}] (e) at (19.5, -1.5) {};
				
				\node[vertices,label = above:{{\tiny $b_4$}}] (b) at (21, 3) {};
				\node[vertices,label = below:{{\tiny $d_4$}}] (d) at (21, 0) {};
				
				\node[vertices, label = {[xshift = .05cm,yshift = -.12cm]above:{{{\tiny $b_{(2m-1)}$}}}}] (1) at ($(b)+(4,0)$) {};
				\node[vertices, label = {[xshift = .1cm, yshift = .06cm]below:{{{\tiny $d_{(2m-1)}$}}}}] (2) at ($(d)+(4,0)$) {};

		\end{figure}
		Observe that $\Omega'\cong \Gamma_{2m-1}$. Therefore, $\alpha({\Omega'}) = 3m-2$ by \Cref{lemma:dim_sigma}, and hence $|\sigma_2|\le 3m-2$. Furthermore, $\Omega'\cong \Gamma_{2m-1}$ implies $\sigma'\cap\{b_2,c_2,d_2\}\ne\emptyset$ by \Cref{lemma:3m+1}. 
		
		Since $2m(\text{mod $6$})\in\{0,2,4\}$, we have $\beta_{2m} = 1$, and hence $\mathcal{B}_{2m} = \{a_1\}\sqcup\{e_1\}$ (see \eqref{set_Bn}). Let $\Omega'': = \Omega-\{a_1,e_1\}$. 
		Note that $\alpha({\Omega''}) = 1$. Suppose that $\sigma\cap\mathcal{B}_{2m} = \emptyset$. Then $\sigma_1\cap\mathcal{B}_{2m} = \emptyset,$ which implies that $\sigma_1\in \text{Ind}(\Omega'')$ (as $\sigma_1\in \text{Ind}(\Omega)$). Therefore $|\sigma_1|\le\alpha({\Omega''}) = 1$. 
		By \Cref{lemma:dim_sigma}, $|\sigma| = 3m$. Hence $|\sigma| = |\sigma_1|+|\sigma_2| = 3m$ implies that $|\sigma_2|\ge 3m-1$, contradicting $|\sigma_2|\le 3m-2$. Thus $\sigma\cap\mathcal{B}_{2m}\ne\emptyset$. 
		
		\vspace{0.1cm}
		\noindent\textbf{Case 2:} $n = 2m+1$ for some integer $m\geq 2.$ 
		
		In this case, $\sigma\in \mathcal{I}_{\Gamma_{2m+1}}$. We proceed by induction on $m$. For this, we first define a subgraph $\Omega_m$ of $\Gamma_{2m+1}$ as $\Omega_m: = \Gamma_{2m+1}-V(\Gamma_{2m-1})$. This gives $V(\Gamma_{2m+1}) = V(\Gamma_{2m-1})\sqcup V(\Omega_m)$.

		\begin{claim}\label{claim:al_Omega_m}
			$\alpha({\Omega_m}) = 4$.
		\end{claim}
		
		\begin{proof}[Proof of the \Cref{claim:al_Omega_m}]
			Observe that $\Omega_m$ is isomorphic to an induced subgraph of $\Gamma_3$. Since $\alpha({\Gamma_3}) = 4$ by \Cref{lemma:dim_sigma}, and $\{a_{(2m-1)},c_{(2m-1)},e_{(2m-1)},b_{(2m+1)}\}$ is an independent set of size $4$ in $\Omega_m$, it follows that $\alpha({\Omega_m}) = 4$.
		\end{proof}
		
		We consider the base case $m = 2$. Let $\sigma\in\mathcal{I}_{\Gamma_5}$. We show that $\sigma\cap\mathcal{B}_5\ne\emptyset$. From \eqref{set_Bn}, $\mathcal{B}_5 = \{a_1,a_4\}\sqcup\{e_1,e_4\}$ (as $\beta_5 = 4$). Since $V(\Gamma_5) = V(\Gamma_3)\sqcup V(\Omega_2)$, where $\Omega_2 = \Gamma_5-V(\Gamma_3)$, it follows that $\sigma = \sigma_1\sqcup \sigma_2$ for some $\sigma_1\in \text{Ind}(\Gamma_3)$ and some $\sigma_2\in \text{Ind}(\Omega_2)$. 
		By \Cref{lemma:dim_sigma}, $|\sigma| = 7$ and $|\sigma_1|\le 4$. Since $\alpha({\Omega_2}) = 4$ by \Cref{claim:al_Omega_m}, we have $|\sigma_2|\le 4$. Thus, there are two possible cases:
		\begin{enumerate}[label = (\roman*)]
			\item $|\sigma_1| = 3$ and $|\sigma_2| = 4$.
			
			By \Cref{remark:al_G_and_sigma_union_v_notin_Ind(G)}, $\sigma_2\in\mathcal{I}_{\Omega_2}$ (as $|\sigma_2| = \alpha({\Omega_2})$). We have $$\mathcal{I}_{\Omega_2} = \{\{a_3, c_3, e_3, b_5\}, \{a_3, c_3, e_3, d_5\}, \{a_3, c_3, e_4, b_5\}, \{c_3, e_3, a_4, d_5\}\}.$$
			If $\sigma_2\cap\{a_4,e_4\}\ne\emptyset$, then $\sigma_2\subset\sigma$ and $\{a_4,e_4\}\subset\mathcal{B}_5$ imply $\sigma\cap\mathcal{B}_5\ne\emptyset$. 
			
			Now, assume $\sigma_2\cap\{a_4,e_4\} = \emptyset$. Then $\sigma_2\in\{\{a_3, c_3, e_3, b_5\}, \{a_3, c_3, e_3, d_5\}\}.$ Using the fact that $\sigma = \sigma_1\sqcup\sigma_2$ is an independent set in $\Gamma_5$, we have $a_2,c_2,e_2,b_3,d_3\notin\sigma_1$.
			Let $\Omega': = \Gamma_3-\{a_1, e_1,a_2,e_2,c_2, b_3,d_3\}$. Observe that $\alpha({\Omega'}) = 2$. If $\sigma_1\cap\{a_1,e_1\} = \emptyset$, then $\sigma_1\in \text{Ind}(\Omega')$ (as $\sigma_1\in \text{Ind}(\Gamma_3)$), which yields $|\sigma_1|\le\alpha({\Omega'}) = 2$, a contradiction. Hence $\sigma_1\cap\{a_1,e_1\}\ne\emptyset$. Since $\sigma_1\subset\sigma$ and $\{a_1,e_1\}\subset\mathcal{B}_5$, we have $\sigma\cap\mathcal{B}_5\ne\emptyset$.
			
			\item $|\sigma_1| = 4$ and $|\sigma_2| = 3$.
			
			Since $\alpha({\Gamma_3}) = 4$ by \Cref{lemma:dim_sigma}, $|\sigma_1| = \alpha({\Gamma_3})$. Hence $\sigma_1\in\mathcal{I}_{\Gamma_3}$. If $\sigma_1\cap\{a_1,e_1\}\ne\emptyset$, then $\sigma\cap\mathcal{B}_5\ne\emptyset$.
			
			Now, assume $\sigma_1\cap\{a_1,e_1\} = \emptyset$. Let $\Omega': = \Gamma_3-\{a_1,e_1\}$. Then $\sigma_1\in\mathcal{I}_{\Omega'}$.
			We have $\mathcal{I}_{\Omega'} = \{\{b_1, a_2, c_2, e_2\}, \{b_1, a_2, d_2, d_3\}, \{b_1, b_2, e_2, b_3\}, \{d_1, a_2, c_2, e_2\}, \{d_1, a_2, d_2, d_3\}, \{d_1, b_2, e_2, b_3\}\}.$
			It is easy to verify that $a_3, c_3, e_3\in N_{\Gamma_5}(\sigma')$ for every $\sigma'\in\mathcal{I}_{\Omega'}$, and hence $a_3, c_3, e_3\in N_{\Gamma_5}(\sigma_1)$. Since $\sigma = \sigma_1\sqcup\sigma_2$ is an independent set in $\Gamma_5$, we get $a_3, c_3, e_3\notin\sigma_2$.
			Let $\Omega'': = \Omega_2-\{a_3, c_3, e_3, a_4, e_4\}$. If $\sigma_2\cap\{a_4,e_4\} = \emptyset$, then $\sigma_2\in \text{Ind}(\Omega'')$. Observe that $\alpha({\Omega''}) = 2$, which implies $|\sigma_2|\le\alpha({\Omega''}) = 2$, a contradiction. Hence $\sigma_2\cap\{a_4,e_4\}\ne\emptyset$. Therefore, $\sigma_2\subset\sigma$ and $\{a_4,e_4\}\subset\mathcal{B}_5$ imply $\sigma\cap\mathcal{B}_5\ne\emptyset$.
		\end{enumerate}
		In either case, $\sigma\cap\mathcal{B}_5\ne\emptyset$. Hence the result is true for $m = 2$.
		
		Let $m>2$ and assume that the result holds for all $2\le l\le m-1$. We now prove it for $m$. 
		
		Let $\sigma\in \mathcal{I}_{\Gamma_{2m+1}}$. We show that $\sigma\cap\mathcal{B}_{2m+1}\ne \emptyset$. Note that $\beta_{2m-1}\le\beta_{2m+1}$, which implies $\mathcal{B}_{2m-1}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{2m+1}$. Since $V(\Gamma_{2m+1}) = V(\Gamma_{2m-1})\sqcup V(\Omega_m)$, we have $\sigma = \sigma_1\sqcup \sigma_2$ for some $\sigma_1\in \text{Ind}(\Gamma_{2m-1})$ and some $\sigma_2\in \text{Ind}(\Omega_m)$. By \Cref{lemma:dim_sigma}, $|\sigma| = 3m+1$ and $|\sigma_1|\le 3m-2$. Since $\alpha({\Omega_m}) = 4$ by \Cref{claim:al_Omega_m}, $|\sigma_2|\le 4$. There are two possible cases:
		\begin{enumerate}[label = (\roman*)]
			\item $|\sigma_1| = 3m-3$ and $|\sigma_2| = 4$.
			
			Then $\sigma_2\in\mathcal{I}_{\Omega_m}$, where 
			\begin{align*}
				\mathcal{I}_{\Omega_m}& = \{\{a_{2m-1}, c_{2m-1}, e_{2m-1}, b_{2m+1}\}, \{a_{2m-1}, c_{2m-1}, e_{2m-1}, d_{2m+1}\},\\
				&\qquad \{a_{2m-1}, c_{2m-1}, e_{2m}, b_{2m+1}\}, \{c_{2m-1}, e_{2m-1}, a_{2m}, d_{2m+1}\}\}.
			\end{align*}
			Since $\mathcal{B}_{2m+1}$ depends on $2m+1(\text{mod $6$})\in\{1,3,5\}$ (see \eqref{set_Bn}), we consider three subcases:
			\begin{enumerate}
				\item Let $2m+1\equiv 1(\text{mod $6$})$. Then $\beta_{2m+1} = 2m-2$, which implies $$\mathcal{B}_{2m+1} = \{a_1,a_4,a_7,\cdots,a_{2m-5},a_{2m-2}\}\sqcup\{e_1, e_4,e_7,\cdots,e_{2m-5}, e_{2m-2}\}.$$
				
				First, assume $\sigma_1\cap N_{\Gamma_{2m-1}}[b_{2m-1}] = \emptyset$. Then $\sigma_1\sqcup \{b_{2m-1}\}\in \text{Ind}(\Gamma_{2m-1})$ with $|\sigma_1\sqcup \{b_{2m-1}\}| = 3m-2$. Since $\alpha({\Gamma_{2m-1}})=3m-2$ by \Cref{lemma:dim_sigma}, it follows that $\sigma_1\sqcup \{b_{2m-1}\}\in\mathcal{I}_{\Gamma_{2m-1}}$. By induction hypothesis, $(\sigma_1\sqcup \{b_{2m-1}\})\cap\mathcal{B}_{2m-1}\ne\emptyset$. Note that $b_{2m-1}\notin \mathcal{B}_{2m-1}$. This yields $\sigma_1\cap\mathcal{B}_{2m-1}\ne \emptyset$. Therefore, $\sigma_1\subset \sigma$ and $\mathcal{B}_{2m-1}\subseteq \mathcal{B}_{2m+1}$ imply $\sigma\cap\mathcal{B}_{2m+1}\ne \emptyset.$
				
				We now assume $\sigma_1\cap N_{\Gamma_{2m-1}}[b_{2m-1}]\ne\emptyset$. Then $N_{\Gamma_{2m-1}}[b_{2m-1}] = \{a_{2m-2},c_{2m-2},b_{2m-1},d_{2m-1}\}$ implies $\sigma_1\cap\{a_{2m-2},c_{2m-2},b_{2m-1},d_{2m-1}\}\ne\emptyset$. Since $c_{2m-1}\in \sigma'$ for every $\sigma'\in\mathcal{I}_{\Omega_m}$, we have $c_{2m-1}\in \sigma_2$ (as $\sigma_2\in\mathcal{I}_{\Omega_m}$). 
				Using the fact that $\sigma= \sigma_1\sqcup\sigma_2$ is an independent set in $\Gamma_{2m+1}$, and $c_{2m-2},b_{2m-1},d_{2m-1}\in N_{\Gamma_{2m+1}}(c_{2m-1})$, it follows that $c_{2m-2},b_{2m-1},d_{2m-1}\notin\sigma_1$. Therefore $a_{2m-2}\in\sigma_1$. Since $a_{2m-2}\in\mathcal{B}_{2m+1}$ and $\sigma_1\subset\sigma$, we obtain $\sigma\cap\mathcal{B}_{2m+1}\ne\emptyset$. 
				
				\item Let $2m+1\equiv 3(\text{mod $6$})$. Then $\beta_{2m+1} = 2m-1$, which implies $$\mathcal{B}_{2m+1} = \{a_1,a_4,a_7,\cdots,a_{2m-4},a_{2m-1}\}\sqcup\{e_1, e_4,e_7,\cdots,e_{2m-4}, e_{2m-1}\}.$$
				Note that for every $\sigma'\in\mathcal{I}_{\Omega_m}$, we have $\sigma'\cap\{a_{2m-1},e_{2m-1}\}\ne \emptyset$. Since $\sigma_2\in\mathcal{I}_{\Omega_m}$, it follows that $\sigma_2\cap\{a_{2m-1},e_{2m-1}\}\ne \emptyset$. Therefore, $\sigma_2\subset\sigma$ and $\{a_{2m-1},e_{2m-1}\}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{2m+1}$ imply $\sigma\cap\mathcal{B}_{2m+1}\ne \emptyset$. 
				
				\item Let $2m+1\equiv 5(\text{mod $6$})$. Then $\beta_{2m+1} = 2m$, which implies $$\mathcal{B}_{2m+1} = \{a_1,a_4,a_7,\cdots,a_{2m-3},a_{2m}\}\sqcup\{e_1, e_4,e_7,\cdots,e_{2m-3},e_{2m}\}.$$
				
				If $\sigma_2\cap\{a_{2m}, e_{2m}\}\ne\emptyset$, then $\sigma_2\subset\sigma$ and $\{a_{2m}, e_{2m}\}\subset\mathcal{B}_{2m+1}$ imply $\sigma\cap\mathcal{B}_{2m+1}\ne\emptyset$.
				
				Now, assume $\sigma_2\cap\{a_{2m}, e_{2m}\} = \emptyset$. We consider two subcases: 
				\begin{itemize}
					\item Let $\sigma_1\cap N_{\Gamma_{2m-1}}[a_{2m-2}] = \emptyset$ or $\sigma_1\cap N_{\Gamma_{2m-1}}[e_{2m-2}] = \emptyset$. Then $\sigma_1\sqcup \{a_{2m-2}\}\in \text{Ind}(\Gamma_{2m-1})$ with $|\sigma_1\sqcup \{a_{2m-2}\}| = 3m-2$ or $\sigma_1\sqcup \{e_{2m-2}\}\in \text{Ind}(\Gamma_{2m-1})$ with $|\sigma_1\sqcup \{e_{2m-2}\}| = 3m-2$. This implies $\sigma_1\sqcup \{a_{2m-2}\}\in\mathcal{I}_{\Gamma_{2m-1}}$ or $\sigma_1\sqcup \{e_{2m-2}\}\in\mathcal{I}_{\Gamma_{2m-1}}$ (as $\alpha({\Gamma_{2m-1}})=3m-2$). By induction hypothesis, $(\sigma_1\sqcup \{a_{2m-2}\})\cap\mathcal{B}_{2m-1}\ne\emptyset$ or $(\sigma_1\sqcup \{e_{2m-2}\})\cap\mathcal{B}_{2m-1}\ne\emptyset$. Since $a_{2m-2},e_{2m-2}\notin \mathcal{B}_{2m-1}, $ we get $\sigma_1\cap\mathcal{B}_{2m-1}\ne \emptyset$. Therefore, $\sigma_1\subset \sigma$ and $\mathcal{B}_{2m-1}\subseteq \mathcal{B}_{2m+1}$ imply $\sigma\cap\mathcal{B}_{2m+1}\ne \emptyset.$
					
					\item Let $\sigma_1\cap N_{\Gamma_{2m-1}}[a_{2m-2}]\ne\emptyset$ and $\sigma_1\cap N_{\Gamma_{2m-1}}[e_{2m-2}] \ne\emptyset$. 
					We have $\sigma_2\in \mathcal{I}_{\Omega_m}$ and $\sigma_2\cap\{a_{2m}, e_{2m}\} = \emptyset$. This implies either $\sigma_2=\{a_{2m-1}, c_{2m-1}, e_{2m-1}, b_{2m+1}\}$ or $\sigma_2=\{a_{2m-1}, c_{2m-1}, e_{2m-1}, d_{2m+1}\}$. Note that $a_{2m-2},e_{2m-2},b_{2m-1},d_{2m-1}\in N_{\Gamma_{2m+1}}(\sigma_2)$. Since $\sigma = \sigma_1\sqcup\sigma_2$ is an independent set in $\Gamma_{2m+1}$, it follows that $a_{2m-2},e_{2m-2},b_{2m-1},d_{2m-1}\notin \sigma_1$. Therefore, $N_{\Gamma_{2m-1}}[a_{2m-2}] = \{a_{2m-3},a_{2m-2},b_{2m-2},b_{2m-1}\}$ and $N_{\Gamma_{2m-1}}[e_{2m-2}] = \{d_{2m-2},d_{2m-1},e_{2m-3},e_{2m-2}\}$ imply $\sigma_1\cap\{a_{2m-3},b_{2m-2}\} \ne\emptyset$ and $\sigma_1\cap\{d_{2m-2},e_{2m-3}\} \ne\emptyset$. 
					If $\sigma_1\cap\{a_{2m-3},e_{2m-3}\} = \emptyset$, then $b_{2m-2},d_{2m-2}\in \sigma_1$, which contradicts $\sigma_1\in \text{Ind}(\Gamma_{2m-1})$ (as $b_{2m-2}\sim d_{2m-2}$ in $\Gamma_{2m-1}$). Hence $\sigma_1\cap\{a_{2m-3},e_{2m-3}\}\ne\emptyset$. Since $\sigma_1\subset\sigma$ and $\{a_{2m-3},e_{2m-3}\}\subset\mathcal{B}_{2m+1}$, we obtain $\sigma\cap\mathcal{B}_{2m+1}\ne\emptyset$.
				\end{itemize}
			\end{enumerate}
			
			\item $|\sigma_1| = 3m-2$ and $|\sigma_2| = 3$.
			
			Then $\sigma_1\in \mathcal{I}_{\Gamma_{2m-1}}$. By induction hypothesis, $\sigma_1\cap\mathcal{B}_{2m-1}\ne \emptyset$. Hence, $\sigma_1\subset \sigma$ and $\mathcal{B}_{2m-1}\subseteq \mathcal{B}_{2m+1}$ imply $\sigma\cap\mathcal{B}_{2m+1}\ne \emptyset.$
		\end{enumerate}
		In all cases, $\sigma\cap\mathcal{B}_{2m+1}\ne \emptyset$. This completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- claim-7: body_md -->
\label{claim:al_Omega_m}
			$\alpha({\Omega_m}) = 4$.
<!-- end claim-7 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
Observe that $\Omega_m$ is isomorphic to an induced subgraph of $\Gamma_3$. Since $\alpha({\Gamma_3}) = 4$ by \Cref{lemma:dim_sigma}, and $\{a_{(2m-1)},c_{(2m-1)},e_{(2m-1)},b_{(2m+1)}\}$ is an independent set of size $4$ in $\Omega_m$, it follows that $\alpha({\Omega_m}) = 4$.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\label{proposition:top_dimension}
		For $n\ge 2$, $\tilde{H}_{\dim(\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n))}(\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n)) = 0$.
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
For $n = 2, 3$, the result follows from \eqref{equation:Gamma_123}.
		
		Now, let $n\ge4$. Recall that $\mathcal{B}_n$ is an independent ordered set in $\Gamma_n$ (see \eqref{set_Bn}), and $\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}_n} = (\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{\beta_n}\mathcal{M}_{a_i})\sqcup(\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{\beta_n}\mathcal{M}_{e_j})$ (see \eqref{equation:sequence_of_element_matchings}) is an acyclic matching on $\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n)$. By \Cref{lemma:matching_top_dimension_in_G,prop:even+odd}, every top-dimensional face of $\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n)$ is matched in $\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}_n}$. It follows by \Cref{theorem:acyclic} that $\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n)$ is homotopy equivalent to a cell complex of dimension strictly less than $\dim(\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n))$. Thus $\tilde{H}_{\dim(\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n))}(\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n)) = 0$.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- theorem-5: body_md -->
\label{theorem:simply_connected}
		For $n\geq 3$, $\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n)$ is simply connected.
<!-- end theorem-5 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
The proof is by induction on $n.$ For the base case $n = 3$, we have $\text{Ind}(\Gamma_3)\simeq\vee_5\mathbb{S}^2$ by \eqref{equation:Gamma_123}. Hence, $\text{Ind}(\Gamma_3)$ is simply connected.  Let $n>3$, and assume that $\text{Ind}(\Gamma_l)$ is simply connected for all $3\leq l\leq n-1$. We show that $\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n)$ is simply connected. 
		
		Let $p: \mathbb{S}^1\rightarrow \text{Ind}(\Gamma_n)$ be a closed path. Since $\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n)$ is a simplicial complex, we may assume that $p$ is homotopic to an edge loop $\gamma = p_1, p_2, \ldots, p_k,p_1$, where $p_i\in V(\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n))$ and $\{p_i, p_{i+1}\} \in \text{Ind}(\Gamma_n)$ for all $1 \leq i \leq k$, with indices taken modulo $k$ throughout the proof. It suffices to prove that $\gamma$ is null-homotopic. 
		
		Let $B = \{a_{n-1},b_n,c_{n-1},d_{n},e_{n-1}\}$ and $A = V(\Gamma_n)\setminus B$. Since $\Gamma_n[A]\cong \Gamma_{n-1}$, the induction hypothesis implies that every loop in $\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n[A])$ is null-homotopic.
		
		If $p_i\in A$ for all $1 \leq i \leq k$, then $\gamma$ is a loop in $\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n[A])$, and hence null-homotopic by the induction hypothesis. Otherwise, $p_i\in B$ for some $1 \leq i \leq k$. In this case, we proceed by successively eliminating the vertices of $B$ from $\gamma$ to obtain a loop $\tilde{\gamma}$ in $ \text{Ind}(\Gamma_n[A])$ homotopic to $\gamma$. Let $j:=\min \{i\ | \ p_i\in B\}$. We consider the following two cases. 
		\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
			\item First, assume that $\{p_{j-1},p_{j+1}\}\in \text{Ind}(\Gamma_n)$. Since $\{p_{j-1},p_j\},\{p_{j},p_{j+1}\}\in \text{Ind}(\Gamma_n) $, it follows that $\{p_{j-1}, p_j, p_{j+1}\} \in \text{Ind}(\Gamma_n)$. Thus, the loop $p_1,\ldots, p_{j-1}, p_{j+1}, \ldots, p_{k},p_1$ obtained by removing the vertex $p_j$ is homotopic to $\gamma$.
			
			\item We now assume that $\{p_{j-1},p_{j+1}\}\notin \text{Ind}(\Gamma_n)$. Note that $p_j \in B$ and $n \geq 4$. Hence, we can choose a vertex $q\in A$ such that $\{p_{j-1}, q, p_j\} \in \text{Ind}(\Gamma_n)$ and $\{q,p_j, p_{j+1}\} \in \text{Ind}(\Gamma_n)$. Replacing the vertex $p_j$ by $q$, we get the loop $p_1, \ldots, p_{j-1}, q, p_{j+1}, \ldots, p_k,p_1$, which is homotopic to $\gamma$. 
		\end{enumerate}
		
		In either case, we obtain a loop homotopic to $\gamma$ that contains one fewer vertex from $B$ than $\gamma$ does. Repeating this procedure finitely many times, we obtain the required loop $\tilde{\gamma}$ in $\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n[A])$ that is homotopic to $\gamma$. By the induction hypothesis, $\tilde{\gamma}$ is null-homotopic, and hence so is $\gamma$. Thus, $\text{Ind}(\Gamma_n)$ is simply connected.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- conjecture-1: body_md -->
\label{conjecture}
		For $n\geq 1$, $M(G_{3\times n})$ is homotopy equivalent to a wedge of $(n-1)$-dimensional spheres.
<!-- end conjecture-1 -->
